7. **Meta Pixel "Invalid parameter format for currency" — CLOSED 2026-09-27: Meta's, not ours.**
   Verdict: on the home page our code makes only `init` ×2 + `PageView` with no currency, replaying
   `PageView` does not trigger it, and it fires once per pixel inside Meta's code beside Meta's own
   "conflicting pixel versions" warning — so it comes from Meta's per-pixel config scripts (an
   inference: the captured stack URL was redacted). Our ViewContent / AddToCart / InitiateCheckout
   / Purchase send `currency: 'EGP'` and a numeric `value`. **The one check that would prove this
   wrong:** a real Purchase in Events Manager arriving WITHOUT its value in EGP — look at the first
   real order's event; if it is missing, reopen this with `trackPurchase` as the suspect.
   Evidence as recorded the night before: fires twice per home-page
   load = once per pixel (1611910119460872, 1614877760150035); our code makes only `init` ×2 +
   `PageView` there, with no currency; replaying `PageView` alone does not trigger it; neither
   pixel's served config (`signals/config/<id>`) sets a currency rule; the page declares no currency
   (JSON-LD, meta, microdata); the warning comes from Meta's `standardParamChecks` plugin, which
   DELETES the failing parameter from that one event. Meta also reports "multiple pixels with
   conflicting versions" — the two pixels' configs are built on different releases. So it is
   probably Meta's own per-pixel config, not anything we send; our ViewContent/AddToCart/
   InitiateCheckout/Purchase send `currency: 'EGP'` and a numeric `value`. **Next:** check both
   pixels in Events Manager (a value/currency default, or a conversion rule); then prove in the
   browser that an event we send keeps its currency — fire one AddToCart in a probe frame with
   `facebook.com/tr` intercepted so nothing reaches the pixels, `EGP` vs a bad currency as control.
   Also noted, no action: `THREE.Clock` is deprecated (→ `THREE.Timer`) — part of the Next 16 / React
   19 work (A9). The 3D reflow/rAF timings the developer saw are unmeasured on a mid-range phone.

### Lesson: "the third party accepted it" is not "we sent the right thing"

The Paymob billing sent `phone_number`, the provider read `phone`, and every intention since the
switch went out with phone `-`. Nothing on our side could catch it: Paymob accepted the payload,
payments worked, and the only symptom was a field on someone else's page — no phone on any card
transaction in Paymob's dashboard (the day we trace one with their support), and harder wallet /
installment flows. For every OUTGOING integration payload (Paymob intention, Meta/TikTok events,
mail, and CAPI/ERP when built), a test must assert the payload's MEANINGFUL fields carry the
customer's real values end to end — built from a real checkout request, not from a hand-made array
handed to the provider — and a placeholder (`-`, `Guest`, `01000000000`, `no-reply@…`) reaching
the wire for a customer who gave the real value is a failure. `CheckoutMethodsTest` now does this
for the phone; the same assertion is owed for name, e-mail and street.

### Lesson: cleaning git wipes the stash too — restore first, then clean (2026-09-28)

To remove AI trailers from pushed commits, the working tree was cleared with a stash, the history
rewritten, and the old objects purged with `git reflog expire --expire=now --all` + `git gc
--prune=now`. **The stash lives in the reflog (`refs/stash`), so that purge deleted it** —
`git fsck --unreachable` found nothing. Thirteen uncommitted files of item 4 were gone from git.

- **The order is: restore, then clean.** `git stash pop` (and check `git status`) BEFORE any
  `reflog expire` or `gc --prune`; purge last, when nothing you still need lives only in a stash.
- **What saved it:** before pausing, the agent had copied every uncommitted file, plus `git diff HEAD`
  as a patch, to a folder OUTSIDE the repository. Recovery was: `git apply --check` of the patch
  against the new HEAD (read-only — proves the base files are identical), copy the files back,
  confirm `git diff HEAD` is byte-for-byte the saved patch. Five minutes, nothing rebuilt.
- **Standing practice:** before any pause for a git operation the developer will run, the agent saves
  a copy of all uncommitted work outside the repo and says where.

### Lesson: a browser pass writes real rows, and nothing rolls them back

The Pest suite runs every test inside a transaction that is rolled back, and the compat harness
builds its own scratch database and drops it. A BROWSER pass against the running app does neither:
every account it registers, role it grants, cart it fills and product it saves is a real row in
whatever database `.env` points at. On 2026-09-27 that left a throwaway account, its admin grant,
an activity-log entry, a cart and a re-saved product in the local dev copy — found and removed the
same day, but only because someone looked. Unchecked, the dev copy drifts from production one
session at a time, and the drift is invisible until a count does not match.

**Rule:** any browser testing plans, from the start, EITHER a scratch database (clone the dev copy,
point `.env` at it with a restore trap, drop it afterwards — the harness's own procedure) OR an
inventory-and-clean step: list every row created (accounts, roles, carts, orders, products,
colours, activity log) and delete them before the report, which states what was created and that
it is gone. Row timestamps cannot be restored, so a record of what was touched is part of the
report too.

### C-1 — the catalogue moves to the server (developer's go, 2026-09-27: stages 1–4 in order, then Arabic; ship each as ready)

- **Stage 1 — SHIPPED, verified live:** `/brand/Rolex` 14,250,000 → 3,746,271 bytes (−74%). See S4.
- **Stage 2 — SHIPPED 2026-09-27**, after an `.htaccess` step the deploy notes missed: the API host's
  allow-list named `catalog/meta` only, so Apache 404'd `catalog/nav` before core saw it. The
  developer widened it to `catalog/(meta|nav)` (nav 401 / meta 401 without the key, `/manage` still
  404). Now guarded: `ApiAllowListTest` fails when any storefront call is outside runbook §4.1.1's
  block. **Stage 3 adds routes: each one needs that block AND the server file widened.**
  The header menu, the mobile drawer and the category tiles no longer
  read the catalogue. They read the lookup tables plus core's new `GET /api/catalog/nav` (the brands
  with products, the sub-types and brands per category type, the genders with both names), which is
  derived from the `all_product` rows themselves (`CompatCatalog::nav`, cache family `compat_nav`,
  the same invalidation events as `compat_all_product`), with no legacy counterpart. Proof: every entry
  of the menu and the drawer (203, hovered and opened in a browser) is identical in English. Arabic
  differs only in the gender labels, which is a FIX: the trimmed catalogue copy dropped the localised
  gender name, so the Arabic menu said "رولكس Men".
  - **Prediction that did NOT hold:** the menu scanning the catalogue on every render was expected
    to make filter taps faster once removed. Measured, no change beyond run-to-run noise (±20%). The
    tap cost is the listing itself: the grid, the filter pass and the facet counts. That is stage 3.
  - **Moved to stage 3: the search box.** Its "View all results (N)" leads to `/listing?q=`, which
    still searches in the browser by substring. Server search (FULLTEXT, Arabic folding) matches
    differently, so moving only the dropdown would promise N results and list a different number.
    Both move together in stage 3.
- **Stage 3 — SHIPPED (live 2026-09-27; parity fixes and preflight removal live 2026-09-28):** the listing, the sidebar, the chip strip and the search dropdown
  read core's `GET /api/catalog/listing` (one page of rows + every facet count, `CompatListing`),
  and the cart drawer `GET /api/catalog/cards?ids=`. The (main) layout no longer embeds the
  catalogue. Only home, product/offer detail, cart, checkout and account do (`CatalogBoundary`),
  until stage 4.
  - Contract = the storefront's previous behaviour, bug for bug. `CatalogListingTest` runs the
    storefront's own `transformProduct.js` + `filterPredicate.js`, plus the old ListingClient /
    SideBar glue, in node over the same data for 36 scenarios and compares the totals, the page ids
    in order and every facet count. Mutation-checked: breaking the facet rule, the default order,
    the blank-price handling or the substring search each fails it.
  - Shipped in stage 3 as inherited, then FIXED by the developer's decision (2026-09-27): these were
    bugs, not decisions. (1) Facet counts follow the search. (2) The price filter and sorts use what
    the shopper pays (`CompatCart::catalogPrice`: sale only when 0 < sale < list), so a blank sale
    price is the list price, not 0. 438 products elsewhere in the catalogue have no sale price
    today; Brand Fashion would have hit this. (3) Search reads both languages with Arabic spelling
    folded, and only when nothing matches exactly it tolerates one typo per word of 4+ letters (2 for
    8+, a neighbour swap is one) against title and brand words. A search that matches never gains
    results. Parity reference rewritten to the new rules; `CatalogSearchTest` pins each case and is
    mutation-checked (always-add-near-misses and two-typos-on-short-words both fail it).
  - Browser, stage-2 build vs stage-3 build, 10 real interactions (sidebar, chips, colour, clear,
    page 2, sort, brand page): count, cards, every sidebar entry and count, chips and tags
    IDENTICAL. Search "rol": identical, "View all results (95)" = what `/listing?q=rol` shows.
  - HTML (local build): `/listing` 3.23 MB → 0.31 MB, `/brand/Rolex` 3.23 → 0.30, `/blogs`
    3.09 → 0.12. The ~45 KB target is NOT met: every page still carries the lookup tables (61 KB
    of JSON) plus the chrome, and the page's 24 rows (trimmed to card fields, 51 KB). That is stage 4
    work and beyond.
  - Taps (local, same machine): sidebar 196–238 → 104 ms, strip 284–351 → 160 ms to updated
    results; the main thread is no longer blocked by the filtering. **Live adds the network:**
    measured to api.watchizereg.com from the workstation, ~90 ms per request on a reused
    connection, and EVERY listing request is preceded by a CORS preflight (`Api-Code` is a custom
    header, `cors.max_age` = 0), another ~90 ms. Expect ~300 ms tap-to-results live, against today's
    ~240 ms synchronous filtering on desktop, and much less than today on a slow phone. Removing
    the preflight (the key is public in the JS bundle; send it without a custom header, or serve
    the catalogue reads same-origin) would save ~90 ms per tap. **Developer's decision, open.**
  - **SHIPPED 2026-09-27, verified live:** `/listing` 3.75 MB → 334 KB, `/brand/Rolex` → 304 KB.
  - **Taps MEASURED LIVE (desktop, 2026-09-27), until the result count changes:** a filter
    combination already fetched once answers in 106–221 ms (the query cache, and the CDN: responses
    are `public, max-age=600`). A NEW combination took 295, 343, 579, 580 ms — worse than the ~240 ms
    of the old in-browser filtering on desktop, and worse than the ~300 ms predicted. Breakdown, same
    day: CORS preflight ~90–130 ms; the listing GET ~90–170 ms to first byte (occasionally ~400);
    the body is brotli-compressed (58 KB → 8 KB, so the transfer is small); render ~90 ms. The page
    never blocks while it waits. (A measurement trap fixed on the way: the script's "updated" had
    fired on the first DOM change, which since stage 3 is the checkbox, not the results; it now waits
    for the result count to change.)
  - The preflight is being removed: the catalogue reads go header-less with `?api_code=` (see the
    parity-fix batch).
- Stage 4 (by-ids for cart, checkout and account; related products; home rails; drop the remaining
  catalogue copies and the client transform) follows.

### "THREE.WebGLRenderer: Context Lost" — CLOSED, not a defect (measured 2026-09-27)

The message is the 3D hero RELEASING its GPU context on purpose. `@react-three/fiber` 9.6.1's
`unmountComponentAtNode` waits 500 ms after the canvas unmounts and calls `gl.forceContextLoss()`
(`node_modules/@react-three/fiber/dist/events-*.esm.js`), and three.js logs that line on the
`webglcontextlost` event. That is why it appears just after you leave the home page. It is library
code on the unmount path and does not depend on the frameloop change.

Measured live, 8 round trips home → `/category/Watches` (two chip taps) → home, by in-app navigation,
with every WebGL context counted: at most ONE context alive at any time, each one released when you
leave (8 departures, 8 "Context Lost" lines), no browser "too many active WebGL contexts" warning,
and the watch still drawn on the 8th return (screenshot). The tab's context cap is never approached.

Not tested, and a separate question: a REAL loss while the hero is on screen (GPU reset, driver
crash). Whether the hero then falls back to its poster is unverified.

### Listing interaction — measured 2026-09-27

- **Filter taps (sidebar and quick-filter strip): the same cost, the same place.** Live, desktop, real clicks:
  sidebar median 239 ms (199–296), strip median 271 ms (241–426). Every tap re-renders ~630 components,
  192 of them the 24 product cards, in one synchronous task. The developer tested on a phone and it
  feels instant, so it is NOT treated as a defect. It disappears by construction in C-1 stage 3.
- **Strip scrolling is not a React problem.** Phone profile (390×844 @3×, touch, CPU ×4 and ×6), 20
  drags + 18 flicks: 0 grid renders, 0 long tasks, exactly ONE component re-rendered per gesture (the
  carousel's own position). Worst frame 50 ms at ×6, on the first flicks only, while the chip logos
  load. The whole-store subscriptions (`useUIStore()` with no selector, 33 call sites) are NOT what
  stutters it. **OBSERVED BUT UNREPRODUCED** (developer's decision, 2026-09-27): a fast flick of the
  strip hitches on the developer's phone; nobody else has reported it and emulation does not show it
  (candidates it cannot model: raster, image decode, Embla moving the strip from JavaScript every
  frame). Do NOT chase it now: re-check it on a real phone AFTER C-1 lands, because C-1 stage 3
  changes how that page works. If it is still there, take a phone trace first (`chrome://inspect`
  over USB); native `overflow-x` scrolling for the strip is the likely fix.
- Also left, by decision: the 239–271 ms filter taps (not felt on a phone; C-1 stage 3 removes them)
  and moving the 33 `useUIStore()` call sites to selectors (C-1 stage 3 makes it moot).
- **Measurement trap: a Chrome window that is covered or unfocused LIES.** It reports taps under 16 ms
  (it never paints), and it throttles animation frames to one per second while reporting
  `visibilityState: visible`. Launch with `--disable-features=CalculateNativeWinOcclusion
  --disable-backgrounding-occluded-windows --disable-renderer-backgrounding`, call
  `Page.bringToFront`, and throw away any run whose frame intervals jump to ~1000 ms.

### Colours — decided 2026-09-27

- **The ~7,090 products with no colour are a DATA-ENTRY job, not an import fix.** Measured: the
  WooCommerce export (8,614 rows, the bulk of the catalogue) carries a colour attribute on 7 rows.
  Re-importing cannot recover a colour the source never recorded — do not propose an import fix
  again. The team fills them in from the actual products, with the dashboard's colour picker
  (several colours per role since 2026-09-27).
- **Do not infer colours from product titles.** 1,065 product names contain a colour word; guessing
  from them is right most of the time and wrong in a way nobody notices until a customer receives
  the wrong item. Rejected by the developer.
- **Importer colour mapping: CLOSED 2026-09-28** — data entry, as above.
- Open: the Joyroom sheet does carry a colour per row — backfill and linking its variant colours to
  the taxonomy are scoped separately.
- **`catalog:colours-main-to-band` MOVED NOTHING on production.** The developer counted it before
  the deploy (2026-09-27): 0 products, 0 colours in the `main` role on non-watch families. Those
  families never had a colour entered at all. The command ships as a PREVENTIVE fix (the dashboard
  now asks these families for `band`, so nothing new lands in `main`). Its existence is NOT
  evidence that any colour data was migrated: there was none to migrate.
- **Gold is `#D4AF37`, not `#FFD700`** (developer's call, 2026-09-27). `#FFD700` was also Yellow's
  hex, so every gold watch showed a yellow swatch on the product page and in the filter. Changed as
  DATA in the dashboard (Lookups → Colours → Gold → hex), not in code, so it is in the activity
  log. Consequence: an order line saved before the change stores `#FFD700`, and the dashboard's
  hex→name lookup now names it "Yellow". The swatch colour on those lines is unchanged. Count them:
  `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM order_items WHERE color_band LIKE '%#FFD700%' OR color_dial LIKE '%#FFD700%';`

Full Pest suite: 1753 passed, 38 skipped, 0 failed. PHPStan level 10: no errors. Pint: passed.

| B5 | **Reordering payment methods is not in the activity log** — the order decides which contract takes the money | Small: one `ActivityLog::record` in `reorder()` + test | "Who moved card traffic to the other contract?" has no answer |
| B6 | **`PromotionController` has no storefront-scope check** (audit, left open by decision) | Small | Nothing — until anyone issues a SCOPED grant. Close it before that day |
| B7 | **CLOSES WITH C3 (2026-09-28).** **`compat:env-parity`** — the `[key]` arm should say "proxy disabled on purpose, use `--base`" and needs a path legacy actually guards; the `[assets]` rule should check the media root RESOLVES to the served tree, not that it is written absolute | Small, tooling only | The command keeps reporting NOT READY on a healthy host, and people learn to ignore it |

## B+. Sitemap and SEO (found 2026-09-26, after resubmitting the sitemap)

| # | Item | What it costs | What breaks if never done |
|---|---|---|---|
| B8 | **The sitemap sets cookies.** `/sitemap.xml` and `/{locale}/sitemap.xml` are web routes (`routes/web.php:500-501`) that opt out of Inertia but not of the session and CSRF middleware — every crawl gets `XSRF-TOKEN` and a session cookie. The XML itself is correct (200, `application/xml`, same for Googlebot); Search Console's first "couldn't fetch" was transient | S: take both routes out of the session, cookie and CSRF middleware (or move them to a cookieless group) + a test that the response carries no `Set-Cookie`. No harness change — the body is untouched | Every crawl opens a server session that is never used, and no cache or CDN can store the response |
| B9 | **The sitemap lists `/offers` and `/blogs`**, hard-coded in the static list (`CompatSitemap.php:31`). `/offers` is the deleted legacy offers page and now a REDIRECT (`next.config.js` → `/listing?offers=true`); `/blogs` has no articles | S: drop both from the static list (put `/blogs` back when articles exist). **The harness compares `sitemap:en` and `sitemap:ar` byte for byte**, so this is a new sanctioned deviation (core omits two legacy URLs) + a 134-case rerun | Google crawls and indexes an empty page and a redirect every time; both dilute the sitemap's signal |

## BF. Brand Fashion launch prerequisites — FUNCTIONAL only (decided 2026-09-26)

**Decision, not open: hiding that the two brands share an owner is NOT pursued.** It is understood
and accepted that a deliberate observer can connect them — shared e-mail templates and sender, one
Google consent screen, one image tree served on both API hosts, cookie and dashboard names, the
dashboard's login branding, the shared sending server, DMARC at `p=none`. Closed as accepted: L1's
templates and mail identity, N3b, L6, B10, B11, L1b. **Do not re-propose them as privacy work.**
Already done and kept, because each also fixed something real: the repo is private; v2 and the
payment callback answer only their own host; Watchizer's EHLO is `watchizereg.com`.

What remains is what would otherwise be BROKEN on Brand Fashion:

| # | Item | When | Cost |
|---|---|---|---|
| L5 | **Per-storefront values** — the post-payment return URL (a Brand Fashion shopper must land back on `brandfashionegy.com`), the password-reset and sign-in landing host, CORS for Brand Fashion's origin, and the asset host its payloads name | **Now** | S–M |
| L3 | **Google sign-in per storefront** — a redirect URI (and credentials) per shop, or sign-in cannot complete on Brand Fashion. The consent screen's name is irrelevant. **✅ BUILT 2026-10-19 — L3-BUILT-20261019.** | When Brand Fashion's frontend starts | M |
| L2 | **Accounts per storefront** — design first; built last, immediately before launch, so it is the freshest change when the second shop goes up | Last | M–L |
| **L8** | **✅ DONE 2026-10-07 — a performance / structure improvement, NOT a launch blocker.** The original "🔴 LAUNCH BLOCKER" classification was WRONG, for one specific reason: an **unverified host `memory_limit`.** 128 MB is PHP's compiled-in default; it was assumed to be the server's and carried here from 2026-09-28 without anyone reading the host. The developer measured production on 2026-10-07 — CLI (`php -i`) and PHP-FPM (web) **both 3072M** — so pre-L8's 206 MB was ~7 % of the real limit and nothing would have died on the host. Built anyway because it is better regardless: the listing index and `compat_nav` are built from a lean SQL read (never full `all_product` rows), the warm writes cards in chunks of 500 and frees the index, nothing builds the whole catalogue in memory on a request path, `all_product`/`all_product_image` are retired (410), Watchizer's warm fell 78 MB → 44 MB, and the parity gate + full suite (1940) passed. Measured slope ≈ **11 MB per 1 000 visible products** is kept only as a datum — against 3072 MB it does not reach the limit until ~270 000 products, so there is **no ceiling to plan for**. **Historical 2026-09-28 note (now superseded):** Brand Fashion's catalogue is too big for how core builds the listing (measured 2026-09-28, local). The listing index, the header menu's facts (`compat_nav`) and the `all_product` payload are each built from the WHOLE catalogue in ONE request and stored as ONE cache entry. Watchizer: 698 products → ~1.5 s, 60 MB peak. **Brand Fashion: 7,579 products → 41 s and 206 MB**, over PHP's 128 MB `memory_limit` (the warm-up died on it). So the day a domain points at Brand Fashion, its first listing or menu request dies with a fatal error — and because nothing gets cached, **so does every request after it**. 11× the products took 27× the time, so something in the build scales worse than linearly; it has NOT been profiled. **What it takes:** (1) build the listing index and `compat_nav` from a lean SQL read of only the fields they filter, sort and search on — never from full `all_product` rows; (2) cards already come from per-product entries (item 4, 2026-09-28); (3) nothing on the request path may read the whole catalogue — which means C-1 stage 4 (the storefront's remaining client catalogue copies and the wholesale `all_product` read) has to be done first; (4) warm Brand Fashion off the request path (`COMPAT_WARM_STOREFRONTS=1,2`, item 4's warmer), chunked if one build is still too big; (5) prove it with Brand Fashion's real 7,579 products under `memory_limit=128M` and the host's execution-time limit, cold. Raising `memory_limit` is not a fix: it does nothing about 41 s. Profile first, then decide (1)'s shape | **DONE 2026-10-07 — ships with the next core tar as an improvement; `COMPAT_WARM_STOREFRONTS` stays `1` until after deploy (W8)** | Was M; shipped |
| — | **Ordering:** set `storefronts.domain` for Brand Fashion BEFORE pointing `api.brandfashionegy.com` at the server — `CompatStorefront` falls back to Watchizer for an unknown host and would serve Watchizer's catalogue | At DNS time | none |

| PAY-OUTAGE | **RECORD — the eighteen-day card outage, 2026-09-23 → 2026-10-12 12:48.** Card payments never completed on the new storefront from the flip until the developer's fix: `payment_statuses` had zero rows ever; 17 card orders, all cancelled, none settled. **Cause:** the stored `hmac_secret` in Watchizer's Paymob contract was not the one Paymob signs with — a live test payment was approved by Paymob, the callback reached `https://api.watchizereg.com/api/pay/watchizer/paymob/callback`, and core answered 403; recomputing HMAC-SHA512 over the 20 documented fields with the stored secret gave a different digest. **Fix:** the correct secret, obtained from the account owner, entered on the dashboard 2026-10-12 12:48; **verified** the same day: order `000027` → `processing`, `provider=paymob`, `method=card`, attempts 1, findings 0 (the map's hop 30 exactly). **Not the cause:** five of six portal callbacks pointed at the dead `dash` host; corrected as hygiene (valU left alone — its value was a live Paymob endpoint, reason unknown); the per-intention URL was observed to win over the portal (runbook §4A.3). **Why it went unnoticed for eighteen days:** PAY-TRACE. | Closed | — |
| PAY-TRACE | **✅ BUILT 2026-10-12 (`41e5a58`), awaiting the single deploy — a DETECTION fix, not a logging nicety.** A refused callback DID write a log line all along (`PaymentCallbackController::fail()`, `warning`), but production has run `LOG_LEVEL=error` since 2026-09-28 (set to `warning` 2026-09-26 while diagnosing, back to `error` two days later; before 2026-09-24 the channel itself was broken) — so every refusal in the outage was filtered out, and "nobody tried to pay" and "somebody paid and we refused them" looked identical: a pending order cancelled an hour later. **The defect: a level filter plus two missing fields, not an absent trace.** A warning line on the default channel is a trace that vanishes whenever someone tightens the level; a safety net one configuration change silently removes is worse than none, because everyone believes it is there. **Built:** a dedicated `payments` log channel (`storage/logs/payments-YYYY-MM-DD.log`, `daily`, 30 files, level a literal `info` never read from `LOG_LEVEL` — the `scheduled` channel's precedent); the refusal line goes to both channels and names the check, storefront, provider, HTTP method and the CLAIMED integration id and merchant order id (read from the payload as sent; never the hmac, a secret, the payload or an amount). The test that matters most: loading the config with `LOG_LEVEL=critical` injected and proving the channel stays at `info` (`CallbackRefusalTraceTest`). **Null result, recorded as such:** `grep -c 'payment callback refused'` over the 2026-09-26/27/28 daily logs returned 0, but only the 26th's file exists and it was last written at 17:13 while that day's eight orders ran 17:26–21:13, and those were the developer's own abandoned passes — so zero means *we have no record*, not *no callback arrived*. **Proof after deploy:** a manufactured refusal (`…/callback?id=1&integration_id=4988969&merchant_order_id=PROBE&hmac=deadbeef` → 403 and one line). | Deploy | — |
| PAY-LANDING | **✅ BUILT 2026-10-12 — core `a0dda59`, storefront `932a2d1`, awaiting the single deploy. The shopper is told what happened.** Measured on the first successful live payment: Paymob's server-to-server POST settled the order, the shopper's browser GET arrived a moment later on the same transaction and was answered with raw JSON `{"message":"Already processed"}` on a blank page. That is the NORMAL case (the POST wins the race), and every early exit did the same; the one branch that redirected appended `?payment_error=1`, which nothing read; a successful shopper landed on the site root with no marker. **Rule now:** a browser gets a redirect to the storefront whatever the outcome; JSON is for the server-to-server caller only. Every non-POST exit of the scoped handler goes to `/order-confirmation?order=<number>&status=paid|failed|pending` on the storefront the callback was SCOPED to (never derived from the host — no fallback to Watchizer). The status is decided from the PAYMENT POLICY's verdict, never from the provider's claim (the night it broke, Paymob said `success=true` while the policy refused it). **`pending`** (third state, kept): the amount disagreed, the order was already closed, the attempt could not be written, or the provider itself reports pending — "being confirmed", never "failed" (a card may have been charged), never an invitation to pay twice. **One exit stays a plain 404 for a browser too:** the unknown-storefront refusal, because redirecting it would resolve the host through the very fallback W6/W7 removes. **Option A (developer):** the page renders what the query says — a confirmation MESSAGE, not a receipt and not proof of payment; nobody treats it as evidence in a dispute; support reads the order, never the page. Three states in both languages, `failed` weighted heaviest (no e-mail exists behind it — the page is the only feedback), Contact us as its primary action. Five tests that pinned the old premise ("a browser on a replay is rare") were rewritten with what is now known; two keep their JSON assertion because they pin the wave-3 handler, left untouched (dead on production). The alias handler's own early exits still answer a browser with JSON for the same reason. | Deploy | — |
| PAY-PENDING-NOFINDING | **Open gap, found 2026-10-12, deliberately NOT closed yet (developer wants to see it first).** Of the five paths that land a shopper on `status=pending`, four raise a finding (amount mismatch, terminal order, reversal, deadlock record). One does not: the provider reporting the payment as still pending (`CallbackPolicy.php:142-145`, "normal traffic, not a decision"). On that path the page says "being confirmed" while nobody is queued to look; the final verdict arrives as a later callback. Pending-then-PAID ends in the confirmation e-mail. Pending-then-DECLINED is closed by PAY-DECLINE-MAIL (the decline e-mail goes from the cancel branch whichever callback cancels). What remains open is whether a provider-pending outcome should raise its own finding. | Developer | — |
| PAY-DECLINE-MAIL | **✅ BUILT 2026-10-12 (`a1d6b97`), awaiting the single deploy.** The sentence that is the shape of the problem: **a shopper who abandons checkout is e-mailed an hour later; a shopper who tried and was declined is told nothing. The one who got further is told less. The fix corrects that inversion; it is not politeness.** Wiring, not a new mail: the callback's cancel branch calls the same `OrderMailer::paymentExpired()` the expiry calls, behind the same gate (one per shopper per week, never after they ordered again) and the same once-per-order dedupe, with `reason=declined` on the rows. The customer's copy was already true for a decline ("the payment wasn't finished"); the admins' copy was not ("the customer reached the payment page and didn't pay" — the wrong story for the person making the call), so it branches on the reason, both languages. The link already worked for a declined order (`OrderRecovery::payload()` asks only for a cancelled card order of the storefront). **Timing, accepted:** decided when the callback lands and sent seconds later; nothing re-checks the shopper at send time, so a shopper declined at 10:00 who retries and pays at 10:02 gets this e-mail beside their confirmation, and the link is then refused as `reordered` (`DeclineRecoveryTest` pins it). **Admin recipients:** the copy carries the customer's name and phone, as the expiry copy already did — no new exposure, but a second reason for `ORDER_ADMIN_EMAILS` to become per storefront (BF handover C12/W3). `cart/recover` is reachable on the production API host (developer, 2026-10-12: Laravel 401 with rate-limit headers, so it passes the allow-list). | Deploy | — |
| PAY-PURCHASE-BROWSER | **Own decision, NOT part of PAY-LANDING — do not fold it in (developer, 2026-10-12).** A browser Purchase event on the card landing page would need an event id matching the one the callback already sends server-side (Meta CAPI), or every card sale is counted twice. Double-counted purchases would corrupt exactly the numbers the new media buyer is about to optimise against, which makes it worse than no browser event at all. Separate item, separate risk; bundling it into a UX fix is how it would get made by accident. | Ask first | S |
| PAY-DECISIONS | **For the record — decided 2026-10-12 (developer), NOT work orders; they wait.** (1) Record the provider at initiation: store Paymob's **order id** (the signed `order` field the callback carries, which `resolveOrder` already matches through `pay_order_id`), not the intention id — `initiate()` returns the intention `id`, `resolveStatus()` queries the transactions endpoint, and those are different identifier spaces. (2) Reconcile before expiry: an ORDER inquiry, not `resolveStatus()` as it stands, with a per-run cap on outbound calls. (3) On a Paid result: **settle automatically** — the customer gets the order-placed e-mail, the team gets one saying paid, not pending; this means factoring the settlement out of `PaymentCallbackController::process()` into a service both paths call. Not now. (4) A failed bookkeeping insert after Paymob accepted: log and continue. (5) `payment_method='paymob'` on online orders stays as-is with a comment. Payments architecture (one provider per method, MyFatoorah) stays at A4. | After A2 | M |

| MAIL-PLAN | **DECIDED 2026-10-12 (developer) — the larger mail plan.** Hostinger's limits are per mailbox and not pooled: **1,000 messages per rolling 24 hours, each mailbox independently**; actual use is well under 100 a day. `MAIL_DAILY_CAP` = **800**, `MAIL_TRANSACTIONAL_RESERVE` = **200**, per mailbox, the same for both storefronts, living on the mailbox row in the database with the credentials (W3/W4, option A — Watchizer's mailbox moves to the table too, env `MAIL_*` retiring after a verified send; absent credentials = that storefront sends nothing, never the other shop's mailbox; team recipient addresses per storefront on the same row, several per storefront). **Known difference, recorded, not fixed:** Hostinger measures a rolling 24 hours; `core_mail_daily` is keyed by calendar day and resets at midnight. The two can disagree; the headroom covers it at current volumes. The counter gains `storefront_id`. `META_CAPI_TOKEN` moves to the same row family (W5). **✅ W3/W4 BUILT 2026-10-13 (overnight brief §1.3), awaiting the single deploy.** `storefront_mailboxes` (M2f, migration `2026_10_19_000000`): one row per storefront — host, port, encryption (tls/ssl/none), username, password `encrypted:array` + hidden like a payment contract's credentials, from address/name, `daily_cap` 800, `transactional_reserve` 200, `order_recipients` JSON (C12); on `DASHBOARD_TABLES`. `core_mail_daily` PK is now `(day, bucket, storefront_id)`; rows from before the split keep 0. **One send site:** `StorefrontMailer::send($storefrontId, $to, $mailable)` — registers a runtime mailer `storefront-{id}` from the row (password never in a config file), sets the envelope, stamps `X-WZ-Storefront`; `OrderMailer`, `BulkMailer` and `CustomerMail` all go through it; `CountSentMail` reads the header. **Three decisions taken tonight, each re-askable:** (1) an ORDER row whose storefront has no usable mailbox is RETRIED on the normal ladder and parked as `failed` after `max_attempts`, classified `config` (a mailbox is minutes to fix; an identity is a decision, so W2 parks at once) — a BULK row WAITS (back to pending in an hour, attempt not counted, reason on the row); an account mail fails the mail, not the action; (2) when `MAIL_MAILER` is `log`/`array` the message goes to that transport and the row is still required for envelope, budget and recipients, so the suite runs the production path minus the socket; (3) the reserve is **200** — a quarter of the cap, twice the busiest day seen ("well under 100"), and the same figure MAIL-PLAN already named. **Retired env keys:** `MAIL_HOST/PORT/USERNAME/PASSWORD/ENCRYPTION/FROM_ADDRESS/FROM_NAME`, `MAIL_DAILY_CAP`, `MAIL_TRANSACTIONAL_RESERVE`, `ORDER_ADMIN_EMAILS`; `MAIL_MAILER` and `MAIL_BULK_PER_RUN` stay. **Production's values, read 2026-10-13 (developer):** `MAIL_HOST=smtp.hostinger.com`, `MAIL_PORT=465`, `MAIL_USERNAME=no-reply@watchizereg.com`, from `no-reply@watchizereg.com` "Watchizer", `ORDER_ADMIN_EMAILS` five addresses — and **`MAIL_SCHEME` is NOT set at all**: `MAIL_ENCRYPTION=ssl` agrees with port 465 but this config never read `MAIL_ENCRYPTION` (Laravel 11's `config/mail.php` reads `MAIL_SCHEME`), so Watchizer's mail has been sending with the scheme resolved by Symfony from the port alone. Worth knowing the day someone wonders why; the mailbox row takes `--encryption=ssl` explicitly. **First production run:** `php artisan mail:mailbox watchizer --host=… --port=… --encryption=… --username=… --from=… --from-name=… --recipients=a,b --cap=800 --reserve=200 --password-env=WZ_MAIL_PW` with the password in an env NAME (never an argument; `--show` prints set/not set, never the value) — BEFORE the first order after the deploy, or order mail retries for ~1.5 h then parks. `bulk-mail:drain --report` is per storefront. **Measured:** Pint, PHPStan clean; `StorefrontMailerTest` 8, `StockAlertTest` 15 (+1 per-mailbox), `NoFallbackTest` 4; the 11 files that set `notifications.admin_emails` now set the row. **Developer's answers, 2026-10-13 — all three decisions KEPT, the wording closed:** "its own keys" = database rows, as built (per-storefront env keys would reintroduce exactly the global-config shape A2 spent two batches removing); (1) the ladder asymmetry stays because an order mail has a shopper waiting on it and a campaign does not — that is the right difference, not two code paths that happen to differ; (2) the row stays required under `log`/`array` because a local run with no mailbox row must fail loudly — silently "sending" to the log is how someone concludes mail works when it does not, the same shape as the eighteen-day card outage whose only trace was filtered out; (3) reserve 200 confirmed. **🔴 `ORDER_ADMIN_EMAILS` IS SET on production — FIVE addresses (developer's grep of production's `.env`, 2026-10-13), the client and the developer among them.** This deploy retires the key and moves the list to the mailbox row, and that is the one step that breaks silently if done carelessly: a first-run `--recipients=` with anything less than the exact five means five people stop receiving order notifications and nobody gets an error. So, as its own deploy step: READ the key's current value from production's `.env` first, carry ALL FIVE into `--recipients=` (comma-separated, no spaces needed), then VERIFY the stored row before anything else with `php artisan mail:mailbox watchizer --show` — one line ending `· order recipients: a, b, c, d, e` — and count five; the developer supplies the value, it does not enter this conversation. Brand Fashion's list does not exist yet: see BF-MAIL-RECIPIENTS. **✅ DEPLOYED 2026-10-13 (developer):** Watchizer's mailbox row enabled, cap 800, reserve 200, `--show` counted all five recipients; the three migrations Ran in batch 13; the mail window was one paste, seconds. **The `unattributed` line of `bulk-mail:drain --report` — EXPLAINED, not a defect (developer, 2026-10-13):** `core_mail_daily.storefront_id` is `NOT NULL DEFAULT 0` (M2f added the column with that default) and every row from before the deploy carries 0, because the counter was never per-storefront until today. The deploy day's `bulk / 0 / 1` is `integration_outbox` row 41000, a re-engagement mail sent at 10:05:05 UTC — three hours BEFORE the deploy at 13:24 UTC. Old code, pre-deploy, not a gap in the new one. The report prints `unattributed (sent without a storefront header): 1` until midnight, when the calendar-day counter moves on; from then every row carries its storefront and the line goes away on its own. Nobody reads it tomorrow as a defect. | A2 (W3/W4 deployed; W5 held) | M |

| BF-APP-THEME | **The light visual pass, tokens only (2026-10-14; spec §3–§7; no component moved, added or removed).** **Where the theme lives:** `app/globals.css` `:root` — the `--wz-*` custom properties (colour, spacing, font sizes, easing); plain CSS, no Tailwind, no theme file, MUI present but unthemed. The 25 component CSS files restate the palette as raw hexes (~430 hex literals; 136 are `#fff`) and five of them re-declare the gold as a LOCAL alias (`--pd-gold`, `--pc-gold`, `--foot-gold`, `--acc-gold`, `--gold`). **Applied:** token NAMES kept (no consumer moves), VALUES from the spec — ink `#111111`, `danger` → spec `error` `#B3261E`, `success` → `ok` `#15713C`, `line` → 8%, `line-strong` → `edge` `#767676`, `muted` → `ink-3` `#666666` (was 45% black, 3.3:1, below the spec's floor), `bone` → `tile` `#F4F4F4`, `--wz-fs-lg` 20 → 18 (spec h2); NEW tokens `--wz-brand #F0B429` (ground only), `--wz-on-ink #ffffff`, `--wz-ink-2/-3`, `--wz-edge`, `--wz-tile`, `--wz-sale/new/ok/alert`; `color-scheme: light` (§3). **The gold decision, recorded:** Watchizer's gold is TEXT 44 times, a ground 19, an outline 7, a border 6 (~150 declarations in 19 files); the spec's brass is ground-only and 1.9:1 as text, so a value swap was impossible. Rule applied: on a light ground the accent is ink (`--wz-gold: #111111`, `--wz-gold-dark` the same); on an ink ground it is white (`--wz-on-ink` on the footer alias, the hero's label/accent/CTA-hover/spin ring, the featured block's eyebrow/sale/dots/hover, the transparent header's bag count, the auth panel's rule, the contact card's hover icon — 14 sites); a gold GROUND with dark text becomes the brass ground with ink text (hero CTA, featured button — `--wz-brand`, 10.74:1). **Consequence, listed:** every ink button whose hover was a gold fill now has NO hover change (the fill is ink on ink) — 11 hover rules; the morning's reskin decides the hover. **Raw hexes that equalled an old token value were pointed at the token** so the token governs them (62 lines across 20 CSS files: `#16a34a`→success ×16, `#dc2626`→danger ×1, `#f8f6f3`→bone ×1, `#1a1a1a`/`#262626`→ink-soft/charcoal, `#c8a45c`/`#9a7b3c`→gold); the five local aliases now read `var(--wz-gold)` (the footer's `var(--wz-on-ink)`). **Typeface:** `El_Messiri` (Arabic) + system Georgia (Latin) → **Alexandria 400/600/800** via `next/font/google` (`app/layout.jsx`, `--font-alexandria`), on `body`, both directions, all form controls and headings; the 7 raw Georgia stacks → `var(--wz-serif)` (the token keeps its old NAME); `[dir=rtl] h1–h6` letter-spacing 0 (spec: never in Arabic). **The three checks, before applying type:** (1) the spec's Alexandria is the spec's own choice for the UI, not a stand-in for a client typeface — W2-TYPEFACE's debt is the LOGO's words (Latin Modern Roman in place of a font the client never named); the UI face carries no such debt, and if the client later names a brand face the UI face stays a design decision; (2) Arabic coverage: Google Fonts serves Alexandria in `arabic`, `latin`, `latin-ext`, `vietnamese` subsets (read from the CSS endpoint 2026-10-14), `next/font` built it with `subsets: ['arabic', 'latin']` without error, and `/ar` renders 24,579 Arabic code points in it — nothing falls back; (3) contrast, computed (WCAG): ink on page 18.88, ink-2 7.46, ink-3 5.74, white on ink button 18.88, `#080808` on brass CTA 10.74, white on the `#0a0a0a` footer 19.80, error 6.54, ok 6.07, sale 5.45, edge border 4.54, ink on tile 17.17; the spec's disabled pair `#777` on `#E5E5E5` computes to **3.55, not the 4.2 the spec states** — recorded as a spec defect. **Gaps (Watchizer's values left, nothing invented):** `ink-soft #1a1a1a` (43 uses) and `charcoal #262626` (47) — no spec value; radius and shadow — no tokens exist (hard-coded per component; spec radii 2/8/10/12/20 need a per-file pass); Arabic line-height +0.15 — the spec gives no base line-height per step, so it was left; three inline hexes in JSX (`HomeClient.jsx`, `BackToTop.jsx`, `Nav.jsx`). **Measured after the pass:** build exit 0 (47 s), home page 10 cards in the one rail, `--wz-gold:#111111` and the Alexandria `@font-face` served in the first CSS chunk. | the app build | — |
| BF-TWINS | **The copy-tracking PROPOSAL (A2-RULE's deferred item, due now that the frontend work has started; proposed 2026-10-14, NOT built).** Today 138 of the 184 files in `Frontend-brandfashion` are byte-identical twins of `Frontend-next` and 14 core tests guard Watchizer's copy by path while nothing guards the twin. **Proposal, three parts, in this order of weight:** (1) **A tracked manifest** `Frontend-brandfashion/TWINS.txt` — one path per line of the files that are copied-unchanged by rule (spec §17.1 and every logic file in the map): the cart, checkout and account logic, the stores, the hooks, `lib/`, `utils/`, `middleware.js`, the sitemap route, `next.config.js`'s redirects. A file leaves the list only by a dated line in `TWINS-DIVERGED.md` saying why (a Brand Fashion behaviour difference is named there, never silent). (2) **A core test** `StorefrontTwinsTest` (beside `ApiAllowListTest`, which already reads the storefront's source by path): for every path in the manifest, the two files are byte-identical, or the path is in the diverged file with a reason. The suite is the deploy gate, so a fix landing in one copy and not the other turns the gate RED — this is the part that survives being ignored for a month, because nothing is deployed past a red suite. (3) **The existing pre-commit hook** (`scripts/git-hooks/pre-commit`, already run on every commit) prints a one-line notice when a staged file under either folder has a twin on the manifest: `twin: Frontend-brandfashion/src/Store/cartStore.js — apply the same change there or record the divergence`. Advisory only; the test is the enforcement. **Why not a shared package or a symlink:** A2-RULE chose copied, not shared at runtime; a package reintroduces one build serving two shops by the back door. **Why not a git subtree/merge:** the folders diverge on purpose in presentation; a merge tool cannot tell the two classes apart, the manifest can. **Cost:** S — the manifest from the morning's file map, the test ~40 lines, the hook ~10. **Decision needed:** whether the test blocks (red suite) or warns — proposed: blocks. **✅ DECIDED 2026-10-14 (developer, decision 6): the test BLOCKS.** Built: `Frontend-brandfashion/TWINS.txt` — 137 paths, generated from the files measured byte-identical AFTER the identity sweep (not from memory), one DIVERGED line (`Account.jsx`, decision 2); `core/tests/Feature/Storefront/StorefrontTwinsTest.php` — two tests: the manifest is sane (>100 entries, no duplicates, relative paths), and every listed path exists on both sides with equal sha256, failures listed by name. The hook's advisory line (part 3 of the proposal) is NOT built — the test is the blocking part. **Not yet seen red or green: mysqld was down** when it was written (every Pest test opens a transaction — `DatabaseTransactions` in `tests/Pest.php`). The proof the developer asked for (fail on a deliberately broken twin, pass on the real tree) is the first thing to run when the database is back: append a byte to one listed file, run `php artisan test --filter=StorefrontTwinsTest`, restore the byte (`cmp` against Frontend-next), run again. **Regenerated 2026-10-14 afternoon from measurement: 136 twins** (was 137 + 1 DIVERGED this morning): `README.md` and `public/robots.txt` split (decision 2), `Account.jsx` is a twin AGAIN because Watchizer took the same Track Order change (decision 4), `env.js` and `analytics.jsx` stayed twins because the TikTok change was made on both sides and copied. The red/green run is still owed — blocked by the same dead database as the gate (every Pest test opens a transaction). **✅ SEEN RED AND GREEN 2026-10-14 evening.** First, a REAL red before any deliberate break: `src/utils/subTypeGroups.js` differed — 2,157 B in Frontend-next against 2,222 B in Brand Fashion, content identical, LINE ENDINGS not (Frontend-next's working copy had become LF; git shows no change because autocrlf normalises, the twins test compares bytes). Restored to CRLF, `cmp` identical, 0 manifest entries differing. Then the deliberate sequence: one byte appended to Brand Fashion's `src/lib/env.js` → `1 failed, 1 passed`, the failure naming `src/lib/env.js (… B in Frontend-next, … B in Frontend-brandfashion)`; restored by copy from Frontend-next, `cmp: byte-identical`; → `2 passed (140 assertions)`. Manifest regenerated: **135 entries** (logo.png deleted from Brand Fashion by decision; DIVERGED lines for Account.jsx, README.md, robots.txt, logo.png). The test is in the full suite of this evening (see DAY-20261014). **Fragility, measured 2026-10-15 (developer's question):** no `.gitattributes` at the root or in either app (core has `* text=auto eol=lf`); `core.autocrlf=true` globally and system-wide, local unset. `git ls-files --eol` over the 135 pairs: index `i/lf` 262 + `i/-text` 8 (binary) — **every committed blob pair identical (135 of 135)**; working tree `w/crlf` 262 + 8 binary, both sides alike, 0 pairs differing. So a fresh clone or a branch switch checks BOTH sides out the same way and would break 0 twins; the trap is a ONE-SIDED rewrite the index never sees (`sed -i` dropping CRs on 2026-10-14, an editor saving LF) — git normalises it to nothing, the byte test turns red. **Recommended fix:** the test hashes text files after normalising `\r\n` → `\n` (binary — a NUL in the first 8 KiB — compared raw), so it asserts what git stores rather than what the checkout produced; and a root `.gitattributes` with `* text=auto` so normalisation is the repository's rule, not each machine's `autocrlf`. Not built; the developer decides. **2026-10-17 — the DIVERGED section has teeth:** entries in the form `DIVERGED <YYYY-MM-DD> <path> — <reason>` are checked by `StorefrontTwinsTest` (exist on both sides, actually differ, dated, reasoned, not also a twin). 6 on record: README.md, robots.txt (2026-10-14, converted) and the four V2-READ-HALF files (listingRequest.js, useListing.js, listing/page.jsx, facetListing.jsx — re-twin together when Watchizer's listing moves to v2). 148 twins remain byte-identical. | the app build, after the map | S |

| DB-BUILD-RULE | **Rule, extended 2026-10-14 (developer):** a Next build must not run while mysqld is needed, and before ANY step that touches the database the agent checks mysqld is alive (`Get-Process mysqld` + a listener on 3306) and SAYS SO in the step. Same hazard as "never the suite and a build at once", different second party. Why: half an hour of 2026-10-14 went to steps that answered `SQLSTATE[HY000] [2002]` one after another because nobody checked first. **What actually killed mysqld, from the Application event log (forensics bounded to 15 min):** `11:32:04` MariaDB event 100 `[FATAL] InnoDB: Unable to read page [page id: space=42629, page number=0] into the buffer pool after 100 attempts … the table has been corrupted`, then Application Error 1000 `mysqld.exe 10.4.32.0, exception 0x80000003, offset 0x643842`, then WER 1001 APPCRASH (fault bucket 1199476113278287199); no memory figure in WER, no Resource-Exhaustion or Kernel-Power event in the System log, 7.7 GB free at the time. Space 42629 = `catalog_product_translations` (18,874,368 B). XAMPP runs mysqld as a bare child process (no service), so nothing restarted it and the error log carries no shutdown line. **Reproduced at 12:15:12** by a read-only `CHECK TABLE` on that table: same fault, same signature — so the page is bad on disk (or in the OS file cache, InnoDB's own alternative), not a memory event, and NOT caused by the build (hypothesis dropped; the build's only role was coincidence). Every compat capture and most of the suite read that table. **2026-10-16, two observations under the stopping rule.** (1) Today's 15:15:00 startup carries `Starting crash recovery from checkpoint LSN=673857988` — by the rule, the previous stop was DIRTY (whether `mysqladmin shutdown` was used for it is the developer's to say; the line is the test, and it is present). (2) **mysqld died again at 15:34:59**, as the scratch full-suite run ended: Application log, MariaDB event 100 **`Error writing file 'C:\xampp\mysql\data\aria_log.00000001' (Errcode: 22 "Invalid argument")`**, then Application Error 1000 `mysqld.exe 10.4.32.0, exception 0x80000003, offset 0x64384…`, then WER 1001 APPCRASH — a different fault from 2026-10-14's InnoDB page (that was the data directory since abandoned; this is the Aria engine's own log in the FRESH directory). The error log has no shutdown line, as this install never writes one. Not started by the agent; the checksum inventory of the scratch run was lost with it. The scratch databases `scratch_fixtureleak` and `scratch_fixtureleak_ref` may be dropped when mysqld is back — nothing else references them. **Corrected 2026-10-16 evening: the "16:27 start" did not happen.** The error log shows ONE attempt, at 16:23:50 (pid 24932), that ABORTED: `Cannot find checkpoint record at LSN (1,0x57ea)` → `Aria recovery failed. Please run aria_chk -r on all Aria tables and delete all aria_log.######## files` → `Plugin 'Aria' registration as a STORAGE ENGINE failed` → InnoDB recovered 23 pages → `Could not open mysql.plugin table` → `Failed to initialize plugins` → `Aborting`. mysqld is DOWN and cannot start until the Aria log is dealt with — a state change, the developer's. Because the attempt aborted before Aria ran recovery, **the on-disk Aria log as the crash left it is NOT lost**: `aria_log.00000001` is 24,576 bytes, and from offset 0x57ea (22,506) to the end it is 0xFF bytes — the checkpoint record the control file points at was never written; that is the 15:34:59 `Error writing file aria_log.00000001 (Errcode: 22)` seen from the file's side. Two stray bytes `%%` were also written into `mysql_error.log` immediately before the 16:23:50 line. The full evidence and the ranked hypotheses are MYSQLD-ARIA. **The XAMPP Stop button IS a kill:** `C:\xampp\mysql_stop.bat` runs `killprocess.bat mysqld.exe`; the control panel's own log shows every stop completing within one second. The stopping rule stands, and it is the only clean path. **2026-10-16 evening — mysqld started by the agent (permitted from this brief on; stop = `mysqladmin -u root shutdown`, never the panel).** Aria evidence preserved to `C:\temp\aria-evidence-20261016` (sha256 recorded in RUN_20261016.md); `aria_chk -r` on the 24 `mysql.*` Aria tables, `-o` for `columns_priv`, `help_topic` and the crashed `proxies_priv`; `aria_log.00000001` + `aria_log_control` deleted; `aria_chk -c` all 24 pass. **Start 1, 17:21:26 (pid 25444): NO crash-recovery line; Aria registered; died 28 s later — crash #4, a NEW kind:** the startup buffer-pool preload read page 1 of `storefront_payment_methods.ibd` and got a page stamped space 0 / page 192 (`ibdata1`'s); 100 retries, `[FATAL]`, the same `0x80000003` abort. **Read from the .ibd on disk: page 1 physically IS an `ibdata1` page — a misdirected WRITE had landed in the tablespace before today**; same fault class as the Aria handle errors (I/O to the wrong file). (5) the remaining ~68 are spread over 55 files (`TableExportTest` 3, `VariantPanelTest` 4, `ReEngagementTest` 4, `PromotionGrantTest` 5, …) and were not individually diagnosed tonight; the log notes `1452` foreign-key refusals and `subject_type` too long for `core_activity_log` among them. NOT a regression of tonight's code: the same 197 before and after the banner fix. **Third run, the tree to commit (banner fix + twins test): 197 failed, 39 skipped, 1,872 passed (300,071 assertions), 539 s; the SAME 197 as before the banner fix — the five new tests are the five new passes. `composer phpstan`: no errors.** **Corrections 2026-10-16 (developer):** the dump is a **phpMyAdmin 5.2.2 export**, not a `mysqldump` — it has NO `-- Dump completed on` footer because phpMyAdmin never writes one; the absence is not truncation (the last statement is closed and `COMMIT;` is present). New `ImageUrl::srcFor($storefrontId, $path)`: the default storefront keeps exactly `src()`, any other gets its `StorefrontUrls::assetBase()`; `BannerController` uses it. **Proof, `BannerImageUrlTest` (5 tests, green) + `BannerScreenTest` 16 + `MetaAndTreeTest` 6 + `BilingualExportTest`:** a bare storefront-2 row lists as `<assetBase(2)>/Uploads_Images/Banner_home/<file>` and never names watchizereg; the v2 meta banner carries the folder on the storefront's host; a storefront-1 row (bare or qualified) lists as `rtrim(config('storefront.asset_base'))/Uploads_Images/Banner_home/<file>` — the old expression; the screen stores the folder even for an old client. **Byte-identity for Watchizer:** 0 banner rows on production for storefront 1 (v2 meta, measured) and 0 locally, so no Watchizer banner URL can move; local v2 meta for both shops captured before and after: identical hashes. The storefront thumbnail/banner RENDERS once deployed: both corrected URLs answer 200 on both hosts today. **Not deployed; a core deploy. Until then the live dashboard and the live v2 meta still emit the bare path.** | the next core deploy | S |

| FIXTURE-LEAK | **🔴 OPEN DEFECT (found 2026-10-16; the finding is the deliverable, nothing fixed): a test fixture row reaches the LOCAL database, because one test COMMITS the suite's transaction.** The row: `storefront_mailboxes` id 901, storefront 2, `StorefrontMailboxFixture::VALUES` exactly (`127.0.0.1:1`, `suite@brandfashion.test`). Production has exactly one mailbox row — storefront 1, Watchizer's, real — confirmed by the developer on the server 2026-10-15; the row exists only locally. **The mechanism, by file and line:** `tests/Pest.php:30-32` runs `StorefrontIdentityFixture::ensure()` and `StorefrontMailboxFixture::ensure()` in a `beforeEach` for every Feature test — INSIDE the test's transaction (`Tests\TestCase::$connectionsToTransact = [null, 'legacy']`), so their inserts and updates normally roll back. **`tests/Feature/Manage/UserWriteGuardTest.php:83-85`** then does `while (DB::transactionLevel() > 0) { DB::commit(); }` — deliberately, to prove the users write-guard holds OUTSIDE a transaction; its own comment (lines 67-70) knows that "committing the wrapper commits everything written before it" and guards against a `Shopper::register()`, but it does not account for the `beforeEach` fixtures that ran before the test body in the same transaction. On a database with no Brand Fashion mailbox row (the fresh production copy of 2026-10-14), `ensure()` inserted the fixture row for storefront 2 in that transaction, and the commit made it permanent; `StorefrontIdentityFixture::ensure()` (`tests/Support/StorefrontIdentityFixture.php:56`) likewise UPDATES `storefronts.settings.identity` in the same transaction, so the suite's pinned identity values are committed into the local `storefronts` rows too. The id 901 is consistent: every earlier test's rolled-back insert consumed an auto-increment value; the first committed insert got 901. Also checked and NOT the cause: `CoreTransformCommand.php:261-264` refuses its `ALTER TABLE … AUTO_INCREMENT` when a transaction is open (DDL would commit); the `shell_exec` calls in tests run `git` or `node` scripts, never PHP against the database; `AuditProofTest`/`LegacyShadow`'s `ALTER TABLE fx_… RENAME` is DDL on the `legacy` connection — a separate PDO session, so it commits that connection's writes, not the default one's (worth its own look, not this row's). **The wider implication:** if one row escapes, the 60-item baseline (SUITE-BASELINE) is measuring a database the suite itself mutates — after a full run the local `storefronts.settings.identity` and `storefront_mailboxes` hold fixture values, which is exactly the state `StorefrontMailerTest`'s three baselined failures see ("production's rows win over the fixture" was half right: the row that wins for storefront 2 is the fixture's own committed copy). The baseline is still deterministic — three identical runs — but it is deterministic against a mutated copy. **Fix, not done in this pass:** `UserWriteGuardTest` must snapshot and restore what the fixtures wrote before it commits, or run in its own process/connection; until then, after any full run, `SELECT id, host, username FROM storefront_mailboxes` and `JSON_EXTRACT(settings,'$.identity')` tell you what the suite left behind. Reproduce on a SCRATCH copy only, never the dev database. **✅ REPRODUCED 2026-10-16 on a SCRATCH copy (phase 1 of the developer's three-phase brief; the dev database untouched — its two mailbox rows unchanged).** Scratch `scratch_fixtureleak` imported from `C:\temp\dump.sql` (sha256 `e1e7d977a71b…` matches the brief; 30,851,573 B; phpMyAdmin 5.2.2, "Generation Time: Oct 13, 2026 at 10:07 AM" server time). **Deviation, stated:** that dump has NO `storefront_mailboxes` table (138 tables; the dev database has 139 after the three `bf_bak_*` drops, and 8,038 products against the dump's 8,032 — see DUMP-TWO-FILES), so the table was created EMPTY on the scratch copy from the dev structure (`CREATE TABLE … LIKE`) before the run. **Before:** mailbox rows 0; `storefronts.settings.identity` NULL on both rows (md5 `6c3e226b…` of 'NULL'). **Ran ONLY `tests/Feature/Manage/UserWriteGuardTest.php`** with `DB_DATABASE=scratch_fixtureleak` (both connections confirmed on the scratch name): 34 passed, 33 s. **After:** mailbox rows **2** — id 3 storefront 2 `suite@brandfashion.test`, id 4 storefront 1 `suite@watchizer.test`, both `127.0.0.1:1`, the fixture's `VALUES` exactly; `settings.identity` CHANGED on both storefronts (md5 `a7ecc88d…` and `ddaf2640…`). **Both things the derivation named appeared, from one test file run ALONE: the mechanism is proven and the id-901 explanation stands.** The ids 3 and 4 (not 1 and 2) are the same effect in miniature: the file's earlier tests inserted and rolled back, consuming 1 and 2, and the committing test's inserts kept 3 and 4. **Alone vs full suite:** alone is sufficient — the commit is in the test itself, so it needs no ordering and no other test; in the full suite the same statements run and the row appears as well (on the dev database it did, on the night of 2026-10-14). A full-suite run against a fresh scratch import was made to inventory EVERY table the suite mutates (whole-database `CHECKSUM TABLE` before/after): the suite ran (1,199 s; 311 failed on this older dump — not comparable to the 60, the dump predates the mailbox table and carries production's encrypted rows), **but mysqld died at 15:34:59 as the run ended and the checksum pair could not be read** (DB-BUILD-RULE). The inventory is still owed; it is the phase-2 proof instrument. **PHASE 2 — the options, laid out for the developer's choice (nothing implemented):** **(A) `UserWriteGuardTest` snapshots and restores what the fixtures wrote** — `tests/Feature/Manage/UserWriteGuardTest.php:81-96`: before the `DB::commit()` loop, read `storefront_mailboxes` and both `storefronts.settings`; in the `finally`, after the writes, delete the fixture mailbox rows and put the settings back (committed writes, so they land). Keeps the test's exact coverage (a real commit, no transaction open). Fixtures stay available. ~1–2 h. Fails silently when a NEW fixture is added to `tests/Pest.php`'s `beforeEach` and nobody adds it to the snapshot. **(B) the test proves "no transaction open" on a connection that is simply NOT transacted, instead of committing** — `Tests\TestCase::$connectionsToTransact = [null, 'legacy']`; `config/database.php:46` already defines a `mysql` connection (same host, database and user, driver `mysql`) that the suite never wraps, and `AppServiceProvider.php:181` arms `UserWriteGuard::refuse()` on EVERY connection as it is established, `legacy` excepted — so `DB::connection('mysql')->table('users')->update(…)` runs at transaction level 0 and must be refused by the door. The commit loop and the `beginTransaction()` rewind go; nothing is ever committed by the suite again. Coverage: the same property, the guard refusing a write with no transaction open, on a second PDO session; what it no longer proves is the refusal on the DEFAULT connection's own session outside a transaction — which is the same code path, armed by the same event. Fixtures stay available. ~1 h. Fails silently if someone removes the `mysql` connection or adds it to `$connectionsToTransact`; a one-line assertion in the test (`expect(DB::connection('mysql')->transactionLevel())->toBe(0)`) makes that loud. **(C) run the fixtures lazily** — only the tests that need a mailbox or an identity call `ensure()`; `tests/Pest.php:30-33` loses the `beforeEach`. Then `UserWriteGuardTest`'s transaction holds nothing of the fixtures' when it commits — but it still commits whatever else a `beforeEach` writes in future, so the class of bug stays; and ~50 tests read the fixtures today (`Shops::`, the mail tests, identity) and each would need the call. ~4–6 h, high churn. **(D) run `UserWriteGuardTest` in its own process** (`@runInSeparateProcess`): the process boots the same `Pest.php`, inserts the same fixtures in its own transaction, and commits them the same way — it changes nothing. Rejected. **(E) delete the no-transaction case** — removes the one coverage the file exists for; a deletion with extra steps. Rejected. **Recommendation, for the developer to accept or not:** (B), with (B)'s one-line level assertion; (A) is the fallback if a reason emerges to keep the commit. **THE GUARD, whichever fix:** `tests/baseline/suite-baseline.sh` takes a whole-database `CHECKSUM TABLE` of every table before `php artisan test` and again after, and prints a fourth line — `4. tables the suite CHANGED: <list>` — exiting 3 when it is not empty; the baseline file stays what it is, the gate grows one line. It would have caught this on the evening of 2026-10-14, on the first run after the rebuild (`storefront_mailboxes` and `storefronts` changed), instead of 2026-10-16 by reading code. **THE PROOF NUMBER:** on a freshly imported scratch copy, with the fix in: checksum every table → full suite → checksum every table; the diff must be EMPTY, and the mailbox row count and both `settings` values identical before and after. A green suite does not prove it; the empty diff does. Improvement on the brief's pair: the checksum covers every table, so a leak the derivation never named shows too. **PHASE 2 MEASURED 2026-10-16 (code reads, no database):** (1) **the default connection is `mariadb`** — `config/database.php` `'default' => env('DB_CONNECTION', 'mariadb')`, `.env` `DB_CONNECTION=mariadb`, `phpunit.xml` does not override it; at `APP_ENV=testing` `config('database.default')` and `DB::getDefaultConnection()` both answer `mariadb`; the connections defined are `sqlite, mysql, mariadb, pgsql, sqlsrv, legacy`; `mysql` is driver `mysql` on the same database; `Tests\TestCase::$connectionsToTransact = [null, 'legacy']` and `DatabaseTransactions::connectionsToTransact()` returns that array, `null` meaning the default — so **`mysql` is neither the default nor transacted. Option B is worth what it claimed.** (2) **The lock question:** the write is `UPDATE users SET first_name = … WHERE id = <the lowest id>`; before it the test only reads that row with a plain SELECT (a consistent read, no lock) and neither `beforeEach` fixture touches `users`, so the test's open transaction holds NO lock on it — the second connection would not block. More to the point, `UserWriteGuard::refuse()` is a `Connection::beforeExecuting()` callback armed on every connection as it is established (`AppServiceProvider.php:181`, `UserWriteGuard.php:157-176`), so the UPDATE is refused CLIENT-SIDE and never reaches the server: no lock can matter on the refusal path. If the guard were ever broken, the UPDATE on the untransacted connection would land at once and PERMANENTLY (autocommit), so option B's restore must run on that same `mysql` connection through `UserWrites::open()` — today's restore runs on the default connection, which in option B would simply roll back. `innodb_lock_wait_timeout=50`: were a lock ever held, the outcome would be a 50-second failure, not a hang. **Verdict in one line: option B survives both measurements; its one required detail is the restore on the `mysql` connection, plus the `transactionLevel() === 0` assertion.** **PHASE 3 DESIGNED 2026-10-16, NOT RUN (`scratchpad/fixtureleak/inventory.sh`, `clean.php`):** (1) **the inventory, on a FRESH scratch import, never dev:** import `C:\temp\dump.sql`, add the empty `storefront_mailboxes` table from the dev structure (the dump lacks it), `CHECKSUM TABLE` every table one by one (a single statement for 139 tables overflowed the shell on the first attempt), full suite with `DB_DATABASE=scratch_fixtureleak`, checksum again; the diff is the complete list of what the suite commits, and the changed tables' rows are dumped for the clean to derive from; the script refuses to run with mysqld down and declares the inventory void if mysqld dies mid-run. (2) **the clean, against dev, reversible:** three rules — R1 fixture ROWS (tables the suite only inserted into: DELETE by the fixture's literal signature read by reflection from the fixture classes, undo = INSERT of the exact row), R2 fixture VALUES over an existing row (`storefronts.settings.identity`: UPDATE back to the KNOWN pre-run value, undo = UPDATE with the current value; STOP if the pre-run value is not known), R3 anything else in the inventory: refuse and report. `--dry-run` (default) prints every statement, its undo and the before/after counts; `--apply` runs inside one transaction with COUNT(*) + CHECKSUM TABLE proof per table and aborts on any mismatch; `--undo <file>` replays the undo file an apply wrote. (3) **What the clean CANNOT restore, stated:** `storefronts.settings.identity` on dev BEFORE the first polluted run — the scratch copy of the 13/10 export had it NULL, but dev came from the 14/10 export, which is gone, so whether dev held NULL or a real identity before the suite first wrote it is **NOT KNOWABLE from here**; production's current value is a server read for the developer (`SELECT id, code, JSON_EXTRACT(settings,'$.identity') FROM storefronts;`) and is production's value, not dev's pre-run value. Until a value is supplied, R2 stops on that column. Auto-increment counters consumed by rolled-back inserts are not restored (harmless). The inventory waits for a database that survives twenty minutes (MYSQLD-ARIA). **2026-10-16 evening — FIXED (phase 3 by the developer's new decision: the dev database is EXPENDABLE, replaced by a fresh export; the surgical clean is RETIRED; the production `settings.identity` read is CANCELLED, not owed).** Option B implemented in `UserWriteGuardTest.php`: the no-transaction case writes on `DB::connection('mysql')` (same database, a session `$connectionsToTransact` never wraps), asserts the default is not `mysql` and the bare session's `transactionLevel() === 0`, restores on the SAME bare session through `UserWrites::open`; the `DB::commit()` loop and the `beginTransaction()` rewind are gone. What the test still proves: the users write-guard refuses a write with NO transaction open — the same door (`UserWriteGuard::refuse()` is armed on every connection as it is established), on a session that really is at level 0; what it no longer does is commit the suite. Alone, between whole-database checksums: 34 passed, 0 of 139 tables changed, mailbox rows 1 → 1. **A SECOND committer was then found by measurement, not by the derivation:** `DropCleanBareDatabaseTest::onBareDatabase()` ran `CREATE DATABASE`/`DROP DATABASE` on the default connection — DDL commits the open transaction with the fixtures in it; alone it put the storefront-2 fixture row back. Fixed the same way (both statements on the `mysql` connection, level asserted); alone after: 3 passed, 0 tables changed. 29 other suspect files measured one at a time: 0 tables changed each. **The guard** is in `tests/baseline/suite-baseline.sh`: CHECKSUM TABLE on every table before and after the run, a fourth line, exit 3 when non-empty. **The proof number — the full suite, with the guard: tables changed = 0 (both runs)** (see SUITE-BASELINE for the two runs). | next test session | S |

When the agent cannot measure something, the correct output is *"I cannot measure this from here, it needs a server read"* — and the command is handed over. The agent has no SSH: it can GET the public API over HTTPS and read the local database; it cannot read a server file, a server directory or production's database. **The incident this comes from, recorded once:** on 2026-10-15 the agent reported "Brand Fashion's mailbox row on production, id 901, is the suite's fixture shape"; the row had been read in the LOCAL database (BF-MAIL-PW corrected; FIXTURE-LEAK is the real finding). **Why the rule matters:** the runbook exists so that a deploy is a sequence of measurements, not beliefs. On 2026-10-15 measurement caught 39 MB of dev tooling and a 39-line hole in `.htaccess`; it also stopped the deploy for two minutes over a line count the developer had invented — that is the price, and it is cheaper than the alternative. | standing | — |
